(4) HIDDEN DEPENDENCY made explicit: C's whole value is downstream of an unstated assumption that Axis-4 Step-D HARD-RENAMES action names. If Step-D instead ADDS new modal names and keeps old names as live aliases in the same PR, no central registry is ever needed and B is the complete answer. (5) SEQUENCING fix: the brief bundles the alias seam ('seed lazily, empty') into Phase-1 code; an empty alias table is dead code plus a prematurely-frozen published format. The alias CODE only has to ship in the build that does the first rename (Step-D); only the CONTRACT (X11 format/ownership) benefits from early agreement. (6) D's data-loss path independently confirmed: create_file→fs::File::create truncates (util/file.rs:132-145) — but at startup-only rewrite there's no watcher to echo, so the real bite is truncation/clobber + rewriting hand-edited version-controlled files, not the A1-Q6 loop per se. Net: recommendation direction upheld, implementation/effort/sequencing hardened, B0 added.

### A1-Q11 — HARDENED · _product_ · owner: Keybindings product owner / PM jointly with the keybindings eng lead. This is a product scope-and-promise call (how strong is the 'portable config' claim in v1) with the eng lead confirming (a) the same-OS guard is cheap but one-directional, (b) the full {mac,other} model is gated on R5/R3b, and (c) whether the origin-platform stamp can ride the X2 envelope without an independent migration. X3 explicitly frames cross-platform portability scope as a product decision the roadmap never made explicit.

**Decision.** For the Axis-1 import/export ('drop in a teammate's config') leaf, decide whether the persisted keybindings file must carry a platform-agnostic accelerator (cmdorctrl, or a full {mac,other} pair) so a config moves across OSes, or whether v1 import is explicitly scoped to same-platform-only and labeled as such — and, if same-platform-only, whether to reserve a single origin-platform marker so the import guard is exact (and migration-friendly) rather than a one-directional heuristic.

**Stakes.** The north-star promise is 'drop in a colleague's config and it just works' (end-goals L54). Today the file stores OS-resolved literal modifiers (keymap.rs:970-993), so a macOS cmd-k exported and imported on Linux lands as a literal cmd binding Linux rarely emits — and the debug validator is_binding_cross_platform (bindings.rs:918-933) already treats any cmd-* on non-mac as invalid, confirming this is a silently broken, half-applied keymap. Choosing wrong either (a) ships a portability promise that is false the moment two teammates run different OSes, eroding trust in the whole 'config as portable data' axis, or (b) gates the cheap Phase-1 import/export win on the Phase-2 R5 platform-in-data-model mega-refactor (~330 sites). A second-order risk the brief underweighted: 'import/export' may not even be a dedicated Phase-1 UI — if v1 is just hand-edit + hot-reload (file-copy), there is no import dialog to host a warning banner, so A1-Q11 largely collapses to a docs/promise call plus an optional guard. The decision also sets what the schema must reserve now (an origin-platform marker, riding the X2 envelope) to avoid a second migration.

_Constrained by:_
- The persisted Keystroke has no cmdorctrl/platform field: it stores resolved booleans ctrl/alt/shift/cmd/meta only, so the platform-agnostic accelerator intent is irreversibly lost at parse/construction time. — `crates/warpui_core/src/keymap.rs:960-967`
- cmdorctrl is resolved at parse time via OperatingSystem::get() (cmd on Mac, ctrl elsewhere); it is a parse-time token, never a stored value, and runs from both FixedBinding::new and the file loader (keyboard.rs:199). — `crates/warpui_core/src/keymap.rs:914-919`
- The persisted form is normalized(), which emits only literal ctrl-/alt-/shift-/cmd-/meta- and NEVER re-emits cmdorctrl — so export already collapsed the platform modifier to a literal (confirmed by A3-Q15 point D). — `crates/warpui_core/src/keymap.rs:970-993; app/src/keyboard.rs:180`
- OperatingSystem::get() is compile-time-fixed (cfg target_os/family) on native and a wasm OnceLock initialized from the user agent with no public setter — a single build cannot synthesize 'what this resolves to on the other OS' at runtime today. — `crates/warpui_core/src/platform/mod.rs:668-683; crates/warpui_core/src/platform/wasm.rs:8`
- CORRECTED COUNTS: full-keystroke divergence dominates — with_mac_key_binding=102 + with_linux_or_windows_key_binding=57 = 159, plus cmd_or_ctrl_shift binding call-sites = 141 (NOT the brief's ~181; the 181 raw count includes ~80 integration-test refs + ~17 use-imports, per the A6 verification). cmdorctrl pure cmd<->ctrl swap sites = 140. So ~300 genuine full-keystroke-divergent sites vs ~140 cmdorctrl-representable sites — a single cmdorctrl modifier provably cannot carry the dominant case (e.g. mac cmd-[ vs linux ctrl-shift-{), which needs the R3b {mac,other} record. — `grep app/src+crates (cmd_or_ctrl_shift(=141, with_mac_key_binding=102, with_linux_or_windows_key_binding=57, cmdorctrl=140); answers doc:2156`
- DECISIVE MISSED FACT: the debug validator is_binding_cross_platform returns IsBindingValid::No for any keystroke with cmd=true on a non-mac OS. This confirms the 'silently broken' stakes AND is the only basis for option 1's import-time guard — but the smell is one-directional: a mac cmd-* config is detectable on Linux, whereas a Linux/Windows ctrl-/ctrl-shift- config has NO smell to detect on mac (those are valid mac bindings too), so the guard cannot catch non-mac→mac imports. — `app/src/util/bindings.rs:918-933; design doc A5-Q10 (L974)`
- OPTIMISM CORRECTION: a cross-OS file parses without error (cmd-k is a valid Keystroke), so it does NOT trip the existing malformed-config error banner path (SettingsErrors → workspace banner); the file is also a flat HashMap<String,PersistedTrigger> with NO platform/version field. So surfacing an import warning requires NEW semantic detection wired to a banner and there is no field for an exact OS-match check — the guard is a one-directional heuristic, not a 'one-line' check. — `app/src/keyboard.rs:169; design doc A1-Q14 (init.rs:239-254 / workspace/view.rs:2670-2683)`

**Options:**

- **Same-platform-only v1 (label and guard)** _(low effort · easy)_ ✅ **recommended** — Ship Axis-1 import/export over today's flat resolved-string file. Document that a keybindings.yaml is portable within the same OS only, and on import run a heuristic OS-mismatch check (reusing is_binding_cross_platform's cmd-on-non-mac logic) to surface a warning/banner rather than silently applying dead bindings.
  - 👍 Honest and cheap and ships TODAY with zero schema risk: the file already stores resolved literals (keymap.rs:970-993), so there is no engine change, no coupling to X2, and no risk of the A1-Q12 None-on-deserialize silent-drop. It unblocks the Phase-1 quick win immediately. Most config sharing happens within homogeneous same-OS teams, so the common case works; and because authoring skews mac-heavy, the most common cross-OS hit (a mac config landing on Linux) is exactly the direction the cmd-smell guard CAN catch. The warning is strictly better than today's silent partial failure for that direction.
  - 👎 Narrows the L54 north-star to same-OS — a promise downgrade product may resist. The guard is an ASYMMETRIC heuristic: it catches mac→non-mac (cmd-smell) but cannot detect non-mac→mac (no smell), so a Linux/Windows colleague's config imported on mac applies silently as valid-but-wrong bindings — the exact silent-partial-failure the option claims to fix, just in the other direction. The 'one-line check' is understated: a cross-OS file parses fine and bypasses the existing malformed-config banner, so new detection + banner wiring is needed (low-medium, not trivial). If users later expect cross-OS portability you re-open this and migrate files anyway.
- **Origin-platform stamp + same-platform guard** _(med effort · easy)_ — Same-platform-only scoping, but on export stamp a single origin-platform scalar (e.g. source_platform: mac) into the file header so import does an EXACT OS-match check and accurate banner — and the stamp forward-records provenance for the eventual R5 migration. Fold the marker into whatever file envelope the X2 schema ruling commits, rather than bolting it on independently.
  - 👍 Fixes option 1's fatal asymmetry: an exact stamp catches BOTH directions (mac→Linux AND Linux→mac), so 'no silent partial failure' becomes a real, symmetric guarantee instead of a one-directional one. It is far below R5/option-3 cost (one scalar, not a per-binding {mac,other} record) and is forward-compatible: when R5 lands, the stamp tells the migrator how to reinterpret today's literal bindings. It also leaves the door fully open without having shipped a misleading half-truth.
  - 👎 Adding a metadata scalar to today's flat HashMap<String,PersistedTrigger> means wrapping it in an envelope struct — which IS a schema change that trips the same A1-Q12 risk (read_custom_keybindings returns None on any deserialize change → silent drop of every existing user's bindings) unless a duplex/back-compat deserializer is written first. So it is only cheap if it rides the X2 envelope decision; doing it independently before X2 risks a SECOND migration if X2 later picks a different envelope. Hard-gated on X2 landing.
- **cmdorctrl round-trip slice (Axis-6-Q9 split)** _(med effort · costly)_ — On export, re-emit the cmdorctrl token for bindings whose modifier is the primary accelerator so they re-resolve on the importer's OS. parse() already round-trips cmdorctrl, so only export/normalize changes.
  - 👍 parse() already resolves cmdorctrl (keymap.rs:914-919), so the import side is free — only normalized() needs a cmdorctrl-aware variant. It makes the single most common shared shortcut class (primary-accelerator + letter, e.g. cmd/ctrl-k) genuinely portable with a flat-string-compatible change.
  - 👎 Provenance is gone: the boolean Keystroke (keymap.rs:960-967) cannot tell whether cmd was authored as cmdorctrl or as a literal mac-only cmd, and UI-captured custom bindings are literally whatever the user pressed. A heuristic 'cmd-on-mac → cmdorctrl' export would wrongly rewrite genuinely mac-specific cmd bindings to also fire on Linux. And it provably cannot represent the DOMINANT full-keystroke divergence (mac cmd-[ vs linux ctrl-shift-{; ~159 per-platform-builder + ~141 cmd_or_ctrl_shift = ~300 sites vs only ~140 cmdorctrl sites) — partial, surprising portability arguably worse than an honest same-OS label. Emitting cmdorctrl into files also bakes a token format users depend on.
- **Full platform-in-data-model now (pull R5 + R3b into Axis 1)** _(very-high effort · one-way)_ — Store a {mac,other} keystroke pair in the R3b record schema and thread an explicit target-platform parameter (not get()) through parse, resolve, displayed(), the matcher, and the validators, so a single build can represent and re-resolve any binding for any OS.
  - 👍 The only option that makes 'it just works' literally true cross-OS for every divergence case including full-keystroke divergence. It also unblocks Axis-5 cross-platform conflict truthfulness (A5-Q10) and the cross-platform binding editor (A6-Q1), which share the same missing {mac,other} model. Doing it once avoids a second file migration.
  - 👎 The Axis-6 foundation mega-refactor: ~330 registration sites, a new record schema (R3b), an OS-injection seam to replace compile-time get() (platform/mod.rs:668-683), platform-parameterized validators. It blows up the 'one-week quick win' framing, gates import/export on Phase 2, and hard-depends on the A1-Q1/X2 schema ruling landing first; if R3b stays deferred, this option is not even available to Axis 1.

**→ Recommendation: Same-platform-only v1 (label and guard).** Pick the honest cheap option that ships in Phase 1 without coupling to X2 — but ship it with eyes open about the guard's asymmetry. The code makes the cmdorctrl slice (option 3 in the list) a trap: the boolean Keystroke has discarded whether a modifier was a primary accelerator (keymap.rs:960-967), so export-time re-introduction mis-rewrites genuine mac-only cmd bindings, AND the corrected counts show it cannot represent the dominant ~300 full-keystroke-divergent sites versus only ~140 cmdorctrl sites. The full R5 model is correct but is the Phase-2 foundation refactor and is blocked until A1-Q1/X2 lands; coupling the Phase-1 win to it contradicts 'independently shippable.' Same-platform-only with an import-time OS-mismatch warning is strictly better than today for the common, mac-authored→Linux direction (the cmd-smell that is_binding_cross_platform already encodes), keeps the quick win uncoupled, and leaves the door open for R5. TWO hardening riders a skeptical reviewer must get in writing: (1) the guard is one-directional — it cannot detect a Linux/Windows config landing on mac (no smell), so do NOT claim symmetric 'no silent failures'; the clean fix is the origin-platform stamp (the missing option), which should be reserved IN the X2 file envelope when X2 lands rather than bolted on independently (doing it standalone trips A1-Q12's None-on-deserialize silent-drop). (2) DEFERABILITY: if Phase 1 ships no dedicated import UI (config = hand-edit + hot-reload, file-copy), the warning has no banner to live on and a cross-OS file does not even hit the malformed-config banner (it parses fine) — so the only thing that MUST be decided now is the promise wording: do NOT advertise the L54 'works across OSes' until R5 exists. The actual guard code can defer until import-as-a-feature is built.

- _Residual risk:_ The strongest surviving counter-argument: plain option 1's guard is asymmetric. is_binding_cross_platform only smells cmd-* on non-mac, so it catches mac→Linux/Windows imports but is blind to Linux/Windows→mac imports (a colleague's ctrl-/ctrl-shift- bindings are valid-but-wrong on mac with no detectable smell). For that direction the import still silently half-applies — the exact failure the option claims to eliminate. The clean fix (the origin-platform stamp) is gated on the X2 envelope landing, so if X2 stays strictly flat, v1 ships an honest-but-one-directional guard. If user research shows cross-OS sharing is common AND skews Linux/Windows→mac, this blind spot bites and the stamp (or pulling X2 forward) becomes load-bearing rather than optional.
- _Dependencies:_ Gated by A1-Q1 / X2 (flat-vs-R3b schema ruling): option 'Full R5' {mac,other} pair is impossible without the record schema, and the recommended 'origin-platform stamp' refinement must ride the X2 file envelope (bolting it on independently trips A1-Q12's read-returns-None-on-deserialize silent drop). Twin of X3 and Axis-6-Q9. NEW explicit dependency: option 1's guard assumes an import surface / banner exists to host the warning — if Phase 1 ships no dedicated import UI (file-copy + hot-reload only), a cross-OS file parses cleanly and does NOT hit the malformed-config banner (A1-Q14), so the warning has no natural home and A1-Q11 reduces to a deferrable docs-only promise call. Feeds X10 / A5-Q10 (cross-platform conflict truthfulness shares the identical OS-resolution limitation and the same is_binding_cross_platform validator) and A6-Q1 / A6-Q12 (OS-injection seam for get()). Related to A1-Q10 (unknown-name import warnings) — both shape whether import is trustworthy vs silently lossy.
- _What would settle it:_ (1) ALREADY SETTLED by the corrected counts: full-keystroke divergence (~159 per-platform builders + ~141 cmd_or_ctrl_shift ≈ 300) dominates the ~140 cmdorctrl-representable sites, so the cmdorctrl slice is provably insufficient and the real choice is option 1 now vs R5 later — the middle slice is out. (2) A product call on north-star precision: is L54 'just works' promised cross-OS in v1 or only same-OS team? (3) Confirm whether Phase 1 ships a dedicated import/export UI (which gives the guard a home and makes the guard real work) or relies on hand-edit + hot-reload (in which case A1-Q11 is a docs-only, deferrable call). (4) A directionality signal in user research: is cross-OS sharing predominantly mac→Linux (which the heuristic guard catches) or Linux/Windows→mac (its blind spot) — if the latter is material, reserve the origin-platform stamp in the X2 envelope rather than shipping the one-directional guard.
- _Red-team added option:_ Origin-platform stamp + same-platform guard
- _Red-team:_ Re-read all five cited code anchors plus the answers doc. (1) Facts 1-4 confirmed verbatim (keymap.rs:914-919, 960-967, 970-993; platform/mod.rs:668-683; wasm.rs:8). (2) CORRECTED an inflated count: the brief's "~181 cmd_or_ctrl_shift sites" is wrong — my grep gives 141 binding call-sites, and the answers doc (line 2156) explicitly de-inflates 181→~140 (the 181 raw includes ~80 integration-test refs + ~17 use-imports). with_mac=102 + with_linux=57 = 159; cmdorctrl=140. The conclusion (full-keystroke divergence ~300 dominates cmdorctrl ~140, so option 2 is provably insufficient) is unchanged and now correctly sized. (3) Added a DECISIVE missed fact: the debug validator is_binding_cross_platform (bindings.rs:918-933) flags any cmd=true on non-mac — this both nails the 'silently broken' stakes (design doc A5-Q10/L974) and is the only thing that makes option 1's guard feasible. (4) ATTACKED option 1's 'one-line OS-mismatch check' as understated: a cross-OS file PARSES fine, so it bypasses the existing malformed-config banner (A1-Q14, init.rs:239-254); the guard needs new semantic detection, the file has NO platform field to compare against, and the smell only exists in one direction (mac cmd- on Linux). So the guard is an ASYMMETRIC heuristic — it catches mac→non-mac but cannot catch non-mac→mac (no cmd-smell). (5) Added a missing 80% option (origin-platform stamp) that fixes the asymmetry far below R5 cost. (6) DEFERABILITY: the only thing that must be decided now is the promise wording (free); the code guard can defer until import-as-a-feature is actually built, and the schema reservation is governed by X2, not A1-Q11. Net: recommendation upheld as the pick but hardened with corrected counts, the validator fact, the asymmetric-guard caveat, and an explicit X2/import-surface dependency.

### A1-Q14 — HARDENED · _product_ · owner: Product/design lead for the keybindings 'live-data' initiative owns the surface call (banner vs toast vs phased), co-signed by the app-platform eng lead who owns WarpConfigUpdateEvent and both error surfaces (settings banner at init.rs/view.rs:2670 AND the TabConfigErrors toast at native.rs:114-127/view.rs:2608-2664). The eng lead should explicitly weigh the TabConfigErrors toast path as the reuse baseline (it is the closer in-file precedent and already carries the startup-suppression + auto-dismiss + Open-file affordances), not just the SettingsErrors banner. Default to the toast for the raw-file hand-editor audience unless the product lead wants a single persistent indicator mirrored into the keybindings settings pane, in which case fall back to the banner.

**Decision.** When a keybindings.yaml hot-reload (or, if A1-Q3 defers reload, even just relaunch) hits a malformed file, do we surface a user-visible error or keep silently logging (status quo), and at what granularity (whole-file vs per-entry) and on what surface (persistent workspace banner like settings.toml, OR per-item persistent error toast like tab configs, OR inline in the settings pane)? The error MODEL (two classes: whole-file YAML/serde parse failure vs per-entry unparsable keystroke) and the reload PLUMBING (settings-style: emit WarpConfigUpdateEvent::Keybindings, handle in the init.rs subscriber that holds &mut AppContext, because set_custom_trigger needs AppContext) are largely forced; the genuinely open sub-decision is the SURFACE and whether to ship it in the same Phase-1 cycle or split it from the gating reader refactor.

**Stakes.** This is the positive-confirmation contract for the "the file applies instantly" promise: today a hand-edit that breaks the file is an instant no-op indistinguishable from success — the user sees nothing change and cannot tell whether the reload worked or the file is broken. It matters now because the hot-reload path does not exist yet, so the error channel is being DESIGNED, and the same typed-Result reader split it requires also fixes a present-day silent data-loss bug. IMPORTANT NUANCE (corrects the brief's "makes or breaks" framing): once the coupled A1-Q3 hold-last-good policy lands, a broken edit degrades to "edit ignored, working bindings preserved," not "data loss / revert-all" — so the data-loss prevention is carried by the reader split + hold-last-good regardless of surface, and the SURFACE specifically closes the remaining "did my edit apply?" confirmation gap. Real and worth fixing, but a notch below catastrophic, which is why the surface (though not the reader split) is separable from Phase 1 if needed.

_Constrained by:_
- Two error classes confirmed: whole-file serde parse Err logs a warning and returns None with no UI signal (keyboard.rs:132-137); a per-entry unparsable keystroke logs a warning, skips that one entry, and applies the rest (keyboard.rs:42-54, warn at :49-52). — `app/src/keyboard.rs:132-137 and :42-54`
- read_custom_keybindings returns Option, collapsing absent-file + zero-byte-empty + unparseable all to None, discarding the Ok/Err distinction. Surfacing therefore REQUIRES splitting it into a typed Result; that same split fixes the present silent on-disk clobber, where write/remove do read_custom_keybindings().unwrap_or_default() and overwrite ALL other bindings on a parse race. — `app/src/keyboard.rs:128-138, :71, :89`
- CORRECTED ATTRIBUTION: the keyboard.rs:71,89 data-loss clobber is A1-Q2's finding (read().unwrap_or_default() → empty map → overwrite all on a parse race; answers doc:121,135), NOT A1-Q15. A1-Q15 is a SEPARATE clobber (HashMap reserialization scrambles key order and drops comments, keyboard.rs:169,115). The brief's dependencies field mis-cited A1-Q15 for the data-loss bug. — `docs/specs/...answers.md:121,135 vs design-readiness.md:241 (A1-Q15)`
- No keybindings hot-reload exists: handle_warp_managed_paths_event has branches for themes/workflows/launch_configs/tab_configs/settings but NO keybindings branch (native.rs:74-134); load runs once at launch (lib.rs:2557). The watcher ALREADY registers config_local_dir() recursively (warp_managed_paths_watcher.rs:263-271), so keybindings.yaml events already reach the handler and are dropped today — adding a branch is cheap. — `app/src/user_config/native.rs:74-134; app/src/lib.rs:2557; app/src/warp_managed_paths_watcher.rs:263-271`
- Reload PLUMBING is forced to settings-style (A1-Q8, verified): set_custom_trigger/remove_custom_trigger are on AppContext (core/app.rs:1637,1656); the watcher handler runs in ModelContext<WarpConfig> (native.rs:77). The established pattern emits a new WarpConfigUpdateEvent::Keybindings handled in the init.rs subscriber whose callback holds &mut AppContext (settings/init.rs:225-228). TabConfigs-style in-spawn load does NOT work for the apply step. — `docs/specs/...answers.md:193-215; app/src/settings/init.rs:225-256; crates/warpui_core core/app.rs:1637,1656`
- TWO surfacing precedents exist, not one. (a) SettingsErrors → persistent workspace BANNER, two arms FileParseFailed (whole-file, init.rs:239-241) + InvalidSettings(failed_keys) (per-key, init.rs:251-253), cleared via SettingsErrorsCleared, rendered + mirrored into the settings pane footer (view.rs:2670-2695). (b) TabConfigErrors → per-item persistent error TOAST keyed by file path with an 'Open file' link (view.rs:2633-2664), auto-dismissed on fix via object_id prefix (view.rs:2629-2631), and EXPLICITLY suppressed on startup (native.rs:50-52). The TabConfigErrors(Vec<...>) event variant already exists (mod.rs:63). — `app/src/settings/init.rs:239-253; app/src/workspace/view.rs:2608-2695; app/src/user_config/native.rs:50-52; app/src/user_config/mod.rs:63`
- Per-entry message text already exists and is buildable TODAY without the Axis-5 catalog: TryFrom yields 'Failed to parse keystroke "X" in trigger "Y"' (keyboard.rs:199-202) and 'No valid keystrokes were found in trigger: Y' (keyboard.rs:206-208); the action `name` is bound at the call-site loop (keyboard.rs:39). Only prettier human-readable action LABELS depend on Axis-5 (A2-Q1/R9) — a functional 'binding <name>: <reason>' message does not. — `app/src/keyboard.rs:199-209, :39, :49`

**Options:**

- **Silent log only (status quo / defer)** _(low effort · easy)_ — Keep log::warn on whole-file parse failure (keyboard.rs:135) and on per-entry bad keystroke (keyboard.rs:50); surface nothing. If A1-Q3 ships hot-reload, ship it without an error channel.
  - 👍 Zero new work and surface area. Logs capture the cause for support. At launch (the only path today) silence is genuinely benign — no prior applied state to wipe. If hot-reload first ships to a log-reading dogfood cohort, this defers a UX investment until the reload path is proven.
  - 👎 Contradicts the axis's headline: an instant no-op on a broken file is indistinguishable from success, so hand-editors get no positive confirmation and will blame Warp, not their file. Also strands the typed-Result reader split, so the hold-last-good contract (A1-Q3) and the data-loss clobber (keyboard.rs:71,89, A1-Q2) stay broken — same fix, so deferring surfacing tends to defer the data-loss fix. Shipping reload silently sets a sticky user expectation that is painful to reverse.
- **Whole-file banner (coarse settings-banner subset)** _(med effort · easy)_ — Add one keybindings error event + workspace banner that fires only on a whole-file parse failure (serde Err, keyboard.rs:134), mirroring settings' FileParseFailed→banner, cleared on the next good reload. Per-entry failures stay log-only.
  - 👍 Captures the highest-severity, most-confusing case (a syntax typo making the WHOLE file a no-op) with minimal reuse of a battle-tested mechanism. serde_yaml aborts the whole parse on a syntax error, so for that class whole-file IS the only honest granularity. Requires the reader split, which also fixes the keyboard.rs:71,89 clobber.
  - 👎 Leaves Class-2 errors (file parses, one binding's keystroke invalid) silently dropped — the common hand-editor failure. Says 'something is broken' without saying WHAT, even though the precise message exists at keyboard.rs:199-202. Likely a second round of work to add granularity. Inherits the banner's startup-suppression cost (must be built; the toast path already has it).
- **Settings two-arm BANNER (the brief's original recommendation)** _(med effort · easy)_ — Emit a FileParseFailed-style banner for whole-file errors AND an InvalidSettings(failed_keys)-style per-entry list for individual bad bindings, naming action+keystroke from keyboard.rs:199-202; clear on clean reload; split read_custom_keybindings to a typed Result.
  - 👍 The two-arm error MODEL maps well onto keybindings' two classes and is consistent with the banner users already see for settings.toml. The required reader split also delivers hold-last-good (A1-Q3) and kills the clobber (keyboard.rs:71,89) in the same change. The banner's settings-pane mirror (view.rs:2689) aids discovery for UI-editors.
  - 👎 Picks the wrong SURFACE as default. The banner is whole-file-oriented and per-entry needs banner extension + settings-pane sync wiring; the startup-suppression guardrail must be built fresh. 'One-to-one mapping / mostly reuse' oversells it — settings' per-key arm comes from a separate reconcile pass (manager.rs:327), not the apply loop, so the reuse is rhyme, not drop-in. The closer in-file precedent (TabConfigs) already provides per-item rendering, an Open-file link, auto-dismiss, AND startup-suppression for less work.
- **Mirror TabConfigs per-item error toast (settings-style reload plumbing) — RECOMMENDED** _(med effort · easy)_ ✅ **recommended** — Use settings-style reload plumbing (emit WarpConfigUpdateEvent::Keybindings, handle in the init.rs subscriber with &mut AppContext, per A1-Q8) for the APPLY step, but model the error SURFACE on TabConfigErrors: collect failed entries during apply, emit a KeybindingsErrors(Vec<...>) event modeled on TabConfigErrors(mod.rs:63), and render persistent per-item error toasts with an 'Open file' link (view.rs:2649-2664), auto-dismissed on fix (object_id prefix, view.rs:2629-2631). Whole-file (Class-1) = one persistent toast 'keybindings.yaml failed to parse — Open file'; per-entry (Class-2) = one toast per bad binding (capped) or one toast listing them, naming action+keystroke from keyboard.rs:199-202. Suppress on startup exactly like native.rs:50-52.
  - 👍 Closest in-file precedent: keybindings.yaml is a sibling of tab_configs/themes/workflows under the same recursively-watched config_local_dir() (warp_managed_paths_watcher.rs:263-271), and the new branch lands beside tab_configs in the same handler (native.rs:114-127). The toast path already ships every thing Option 3 must build: per-item granularity, an Open-file affordance, auto-dismiss-on-fix, and — critically — the startup/transient-suppression guardrail (native.rs:50-52) that the brief lists as a needed addition. It is the only option that gives the hand-editor BOTH that-it-failed and which binding, at the lowest marginal cost, while keeping the forced settings-style apply plumbing. Per-entry messages are buildable today (no Axis-5 dependency for a functional message).
  - 👎 Toast noise if many entries are bad (mitigate: cap count or fold per-entry into one toast). Toasts are less 'sticky' than a banner and don't mirror into the settings pane footer — a discoverability loss for users who edit via the UI (but the file-watching live-data audience edits the raw file, not the pane). Introduces a second config-error surface style alongside settings.toml's banner (the residual-risk counter). Still needs the reader split, a new event variant, a failed-entry collection threaded through the apply loop, and a toast subscriber cloned from subscribe_to_tab_config_errors.
- **Phased: reader-split + hold-last-good now, surface deferred to Phase 1.x** _(low effort · easy)_ — In Phase 1 do only the load-bearing, gating work: split read_custom_keybindings into a typed Result, fix the keyboard.rs:71,89 data-loss clobber, and implement hold-last-good on parse error (A1-Q2/Q3). Keep log-only surfacing initially; add the toast/banner in a fast-follow once the typed error channel exists.
  - 👍 Decouples the genuinely Phase-1-gating refactor (reader split, which A1-Q3 needs anyway) from the additive UX surface. Fixes the real data-loss bug and de-fangs the worst failure mode (hold-last-good preserves working bindings) immediately, at lower risk, while the surface — which can be retrofitted with no rework because the error is already typed — slips if schedule demands. Directly refutes the brief's 'must specify the surface before the handler is written' claim.
  - 👎 Ships a reload that still can't positively confirm 'your edit applied' — the confirmation gap stays open during the gap. Given the TabConfigs toast makes the surface cheap, the savings from deferring are small and you risk the foreclosure problem (users form a silent-reload expectation). Best treated as a fallback if Phase 1 is over-subscribed, not the default.
- **Inline-in-settings-editor surfacing** _(high effort · easy)_ — Surface parse/validation errors inline in the keybindings settings UI next to the offending entry, where the user edits.
  - 👍 Highest-fidelity, lowest-noise for UI-editors: error appears exactly where it's fixed, no global clutter, natural per-entry granularity, no startup-suppression needed.
  - 👎 Depends on an Axis-5 settings-UI surface that may not exist yet, so it cannot be the launch answer for raw-file hand-editors (the live-data audience, who may never open the pane). Highest effort; does nothing for the file-watcher path unless paired with a toast/banner anyway. A later layer on top of the toast/banner contract, not a substitute.

**→ Recommendation: Mirror TabConfigs per-item error toast (settings-style reload plumbing) — RECOMMENDED.** Silence (Option 1) is disqualified by the axis's premise. Among the surfacing options, the deciding facts are: (1) keybindings.yaml is a sibling of tab_configs under the same recursively-watched config_local_dir() and its watcher branch lands beside tab_configs in native.rs, so the TabConfigs surface is the CLOSEST in-file precedent — not settings; (2) the TabConfigErrors toast path already ships every capability Option 3's banner would have to build: per-item granularity, an 'Open file' link, auto-dismiss-on-fix, and the startup/transient-suppression guardrail (native.rs:50-52) that the brief itself flagged as a required addition; (3) the per-entry message ('binding <name>: failed to parse keystroke X') is buildable today from keyboard.rs:199-202 with no Axis-5 dependency, so the 'tells the user WHICH binding broke' requirement is satisfiable now. The reload PLUMBING is independently forced to settings-style by A1-Q8 (set_custom_trigger needs &mut AppContext), but that does not dictate the SURFACE — the failed-entries vector rides the new event and renders as a toast just as easily as a banner. So the recommendation keeps the forced settings-style apply plumbing but corrects the surface from the settings banner to the TabConfigs toast, which is lower marginal cost and better per-entry UX. On deferability: the SURFACE is separable from the gating reader split (owned by A1-Q3), so it is not a hard Phase-1 blocker; given the toast makes it cheap, ship it in the same cycle, but the phased option is a clean fallback if Phase 1 is over-subscribed. The whole question is moot if A1-Q3 declines to build hot-reload in Phase 1, since launch-only silence is benign. Guardrail (now free): reuse native.rs:50-52's startup-suppression and the object_id auto-dismiss so transient mid-save edits don't flash toasts.

- _Residual risk:_ The strongest surviving counter is UX consistency: settings.toml already trained users that 'a managed config file failed to parse' shows up as a persistent workspace BANNER (with a settings-pane footer mirror). Introducing a toast for keybindings.yaml means two different broken-file affordances for two config files, and a skeptic could argue Warp should pick ONE app-wide surface and, since settings owns the banner, keybindings should join it — accepting the extra wiring and the lost startup-suppression-for-free. (Counter-counter: TabConfigs ALREADY established the toast as the surface for warp-managed-paths-watched files, which keybindings.yaml IS — so the consistency argument actually splits, and the toast is the more consistent choice for files under that watcher. But the banner-consistency argument is the honest strongest residual.) A second residual: toasts can be missed or are noisier than a banner when many entries are bad; if real usage shows >2-3 simultaneous bad bindings is common, fold per-entry into a single toast or reconsider the banner. Third: with hold-last-good already de-fanging data loss, a reviewer could reasonably argue the whole surface is a fast-follow, not Phase 1 (the phased option) — defensible if schedule is tight.
- _Dependencies:_ GATED by A1-Q3 (is keybindings hot-reload built in Phase 1 at all — none exists today, native.rs:74-134 has no branch; at launch-only, silence is benign so this whole question defers with it). The reload PLUMBING is FORCED to settings-style by A1-Q8 (set_custom_trigger needs &mut AppContext; emit WarpConfigUpdateEvent::Keybindings, handle in init.rs subscriber) — but the error SURFACE (toast vs banner) is ORTHOGONAL to that and is the open sub-decision. Tightly coupled to A1-Q2/Q3: surfacing the error and holding last-good are the SAME typed-Result split of read_custom_keybindings (keyboard.rs:128-138), which ALSO fixes the in-app data-loss clobber at keyboard.rs:71,89 — that clobber is A1-Q2's finding (NOT A1-Q15, which is the separate formatting/ordering clobber; the brief mis-cited this). The SURFACE is separable from the gating reader split, so it is NOT a hard Phase-1 blocker (the phased option). Gated for validation by A1-Q13 (the file path is cfg(test)-stubbed; a test seam is needed). Per-entry message quality has only a SOFT dependency on Axis-5 (A2-Q1/R9): a functional message is buildable today from keyboard.rs:199-202; only prettier action labels need the catalog. Option 6 (inline) depends on the Axis-5 settings UI.
- _What would settle it:_ Two cheap probes. (1) The brief's dogfood test, kept: hand 3-5 power users a deliberately broken keybindings.yaml under live hot-reload — one with a whole-file YAML syntax error (Class 1) and one with a single invalid keystroke string (Class 2) — and watch whether they can tell the reload failed and locate the cause with NO surfacing. If they cannot self-diagnose (expected), surfacing is mandatory and only granularity/surface remain open. (2) ADD a surface-reuse eng spike comparing TWO paths head-to-head in net lines: (a) clone subscribe_to_tab_config_errors into a keybindings toast subscriber + add a KeybindingsErrors(Vec<...>) event modeled on TabConfigErrors, vs (b) extend SettingsErrors with a keybindings arm + settings-pane sync. Whichever is fewer lines AND the product lead's banner-vs-toast consistency call settle the surface; the spike should confirm the toast path inherits native.rs:50-52 startup-suppression and view.rs:2629-2631 auto-dismiss for free (expected). (3) Confirm with A1-Q3 whether hot-reload is in Phase 1 — if not, defer A1-Q14 with it.
- _Red-team added option:_ Mirror TabConfigs per-item error toast (settings-style reload plumbing)
- _Red-team:_ Core direction UPHELD: silence (Option 1) is disqualified, surfacing both error classes is right, and the typed-Result reader split is the genuine enabling refactor that also kills the live data-loss clobber. But the brief's recommended IMPLEMENTATION was attacked and corrected on four points:

1) MISSING/BETTER PRECEDENT (the big one). The brief recommends mirroring SETTINGS (a persistent workspace banner) and treats that as the only reuse target. There is a SECOND, closer-fit precedent it never cites: TabConfigErrors. keybindings.yaml lives in config_local_dir() — the same directory the WarpManagedPathsWatcher already watches recursively (warp_managed_paths_watcher.rs:263-271), so its events already reach handle_warp_managed_paths_event and are simply dropped today. The keybindings watcher branch must be added right beside the tab_configs branch (native.rs:114-127). TabConfigs renders errors as PER-ITEM persistent error TOASTS keyed by file path, each with an "Open file" link (view.rs:2633-2664), auto-dismissed when the file is fixed (object_id prefix, view.rs:2629-2631), and EXPLICITLY suppressed on startup (native.rs:50-52) — which is exactly the "only surface on a settled reload, not transient/startup" guardrail the brief had to invent from scratch. That guardrail is already written for the toast path and is NOT free on the banner path. So I added "Mirror TabConfigs per-item error toast" as an option and made it the recommendation. The settings two-arm contract is the right ERROR MODEL (whole-file vs per-entry) but the wrong default SURFACE.

2) PLUMBING vs SURFACE conflation (HIDDEN DEPENDENCY made explicit). The brief's Option 3 fuses "reuse the settings two-arm" (the error model) with "use the settings banner" (the surface). Per A1-Q8 (verified at answers doc:193-215), the reload PLUMBING is FORCED to be settings-style — emit a new WarpConfigUpdateEvent::Keybindings, handle it in the init.rs-side subscriber that holds &mut AppContext — because set_custom_trigger/remove_custom_trigger (core/app.rs:1637,1656) need AppContext, which the native.rs handler (ModelContext<WarpConfig>) only reaches by deref. TabConfigs-style in-spawn load does NOT work for the apply step. BUT the SURFACE (toast vs banner) is orthogonal to that: the failed-entries vector rides the event and can be rendered either way. The brief silently assumed banner because it assumed settings-style plumbing implies settings-style surface. It does not.

3) DEFERABILITY OVERSTATED. The brief asserts surfacing "must be specified before the reload handler is written, not retrofitted." Half true. The reader SPLIT is genuinely gating (A1-Q2/Q3 need it for hold-last-good and to fix the clobber). The error SURFACE is additive: once the reader returns a typed error and the apply loop collects failed entries, a toast/banner can be wired in a later PR with no retrofit pain. So I added a phased option (reader-split + hold-last-good now, surface in Phase 1.x). It is genuinely separable, so A1-Q14 is NOT a hard Phase-1 blocker — the blocker is the reader split, owned by A1-Q3. Net: surfacing is cheap enough (given the TabConfigs toast) that doing it in the same cycle is best, but if schedule slips it can defer without lock-in. Also: the entire question is contingent on A1-Q3 building hot-reload in Phase 1 at all; at launch-only, silence is benign (the brief concedes this).

4) STAKES SLIGHTLY INFLATED. "Makes or breaks the live-data promise" overstates it once the coupled A1-Q3 hold-last-good policy is priced in: with hold-last-good, a broken hand-edit degrades to "edit silently ignored, working bindings preserved," NOT "data loss / revert-all." Surfacing then closes the "did my edit actually apply?" positive-confirmation gap — real and worth fixing, but a notch below catastrophic. The data-loss prevention comes from the reader split + hold-last-good (A1-Q2/Q3), which happen regardless of the surface choice.

Minor corrections: (a) The brief's dependencies field attributes the keyboard.rs:71,89 silent clobber to A1-Q15. That is a mis-citation — keyboard.rs:71,89 is the read().unwrap_or_default() data-loss bug, which is A1-Q2's finding (answers doc:121,135). A1-Q15 is a SEPARATE formatting/ordering clobber (HashMap reserialization). (b) The brief overstates the Axis-5 coupling for per-entry message quality: the offending keystroke string and trigger are already in the error text (keyboard.rs:199-202,206-208) and the action `name` is in scope at the call-site loop (keyboard.rs:39), so a usable per-entry message ("binding <name>: failed to parse keystroke X") is buildable TODAY; only prettier human-readable action LABELS depend on the Axis-5 catalog. (c) The "one-to-one mapping / mostly reuse" framing for settings is rhyme, not literal reuse — settings' per-key arm comes from a separate reconcile pass (reload_all_public_settings returns failed_keys, manager.rs:327), whereas keybindings' per-entry failures are collected inside the apply loop; the closer literal reuse is TabConfigs, which already returns (configs, errors) as a tuple.

All five original code_grounding facts in the brief verified true against keyboard.rs, native.rs, lib.rs, settings/init.rs, view.rs, and the answers doc.

### A1-Q15 — HARDENED · _product_ · owner: Product owner for the keybindings overhaul owns the contract framing ('is comment loss acceptable / what do we promise about ordering and comments'), tagged user-product-decision, design doc L245. The keybindings eng-lead owns the implementation tier and two specific technical calls the brief glossed: (1) IndexMap-vs-BTreeMap-vs-CST cost/behavior split, and (2) the correctness detail that keyboard.rs:91 must use .shift_remove() (not the deprecated .remove()/swap_remove) for the reset/unbind path to actually preserve order. Eng-lead also owns adopting the A1-Q5 atomic write alongside, since that is the only genuine bug on the path.

**Decision.** For keybindings.yaml — a file Warp positions as a portable, hand-editable, version-controlled source of truth — decide what the 'round-trip safe' promise covers when an in-app edit rewrites the file. Two independent knobs hide in one question: (a) ORDERING — does a one-binding edit preserve stable, deterministic key order (reviewable one-line git diffs) or reshuffle the whole file nondeterministically; and (b) COMMENTS — are hand-written comments preserved across an in-app save or silently dropped. Decide each separately and bundle the unrelated-but-co-located A1-Q5 atomic-write fix, which is the only runtime data-loss bug on this path.

**Stakes.** Axis 1's headline end-state is 'the keymap is a hand-editable, shareable file that is the source of truth' (design doc L125, end-goals L47). Today every in-app rebind/unbind does a full-file read-modify-write of an unordered HashMap via serde_yaml::to_writer (keyboard.rs:71,89,115,169), so a single one-binding change (a) re-orders the WHOLE file nondeterministically — turning a one-line semantic change into an every-line git diff that is unreviewable in version control and merge-conflict-prone — and (b) silently discards any comments the user hand-wrote. CORRECTED IMPACT FRAMING: because there is no hot-reload watcher for keybindings.yaml (native.rs:74-134; the file is read once at startup, lib.rs:2557), the ordering/comment harm manifests as VCS-diff and trust damage, NOT runtime data loss. The genuine runtime data-loss on this same path is separate: a parse-failure/half-written-file race makes read_custom_keybindings return None -> unwrap_or_default() = empty map -> save writes back only the one edited binding, clobbering all others (A1-Q5 #5, A1-Q12); that is fixed by the A1-Q5 atomic temp+rename write independent of this decision. The choice also sets how much engineering Axis 1 carries (cheap ordering swap) vs. defers to the Axis-2 schema work (comment fidelity), and is entangled with the unresolved A1-Q1 ruling on whether R3b's record schema rebuilds the serializer.

_Constrained by:_
- CustomKeybindings is a newtype over std::collections::HashMap<String, PersistedTrigger>, so serialization iteration order is nondeterministic — every save can re-order every key. It is the sole on-disk structure (one reader read_custom_keybindings, one writer save_custom_keybindings), so routing all reads/writes through an ordered map is trivially consistent. — `app/src/keyboard.rs:169 (struct); :38,:71,:89,:103,:128 (sole reader/writer call sites)`
- Save re-serializes the whole map with serde_yaml::to_writer; serde_yaml 0.8 deserializes into typed structs with no comment-preserving (CST) round-trip, so all user comments are discarded on every write. serde_yaml is also itself unmaintained/deprecated (Cargo.lock carries serde_yaml 0.9.34+deprecated alongside the 0.8.26 the app uses), strengthening the case against building a comment-preserving writer on top of it. — `app/src/keyboard.rs:103-122 (to_writer :115); root Cargo.toml serde_yaml="0.8"; app/Cargo.toml:190; Cargo.lock (serde_yaml 0.8.26 and 0.9.34+deprecated)`
- Every in-app edit is a full-file read-modify-write: write_custom_keybinding/remove_custom_keybinding call read_custom_keybindings().unwrap_or_default(), mutate ONE entry, then save_custom_keybindings(map) rewriting the entire file from the in-memory map. Reachable from the live settings UI: rebind (bindings.rs:496), reset-to-default which deletes the key (bindings.rs:513 -> keyboard.rs:91), and unbind which INSERTs 'none' (keybindings.rs:600). — `app/src/keyboard.rs:71,73-74,89,91-92; app/src/util/bindings.rs:496,513; app/src/settings_view/keybindings.rs:600`
- On-disk format is a flat top-level YAML map of name -> single-line string trigger (PersistedTrigger wraps a String); no nesting. An ordered-map swap therefore yields the identical YAML shape. CORRECTION to the brief's 'drop-in type swap': it is a type swap PLUS one required call-site change — keyboard.rs:91 map.0.remove(name) is IndexMap::remove (swap_remove semantics, relocates the last entry, deprecated in indexmap 2.x) and must become .shift_remove() to actually preserve order on the reset/unbind-to-default path; otherwise that path still emits a 2-line reshuffle diff. — `app/src/keyboard.rs:155-169,174 (schema); :91 (remove call site needing shift_remove); Cargo.lock indexmap 2.12.0`
- indexmap with the serde feature is ALREADY a workspace/app dependency — no Cargo.toml change is needed to adopt IndexMap. The brief implied the serde feature would be added; it is present today. — `app/Cargo.toml:133 (indexmap = { version = "2.0.2", features = ["serde"] }); Cargo.lock indexmap 2.12.0`
- The write is a non-atomic truncating fs::File::create, and a parse-failure mid-write already silently clobbers all other bindings via unwrap_or_default — formatting and the (separate) runtime data-loss risk share the same path. This data-loss is the A1-Q5 atomic temp+rename fix and is independent of the ordering/comment decision. There is NO hot-reload watcher for keybindings.yaml, so order preservation is a VCS-diff concern today, not a live-merge concern. — `app/src/util/file.rs:132-145 (:143 fs::File::create); app/src/keyboard.rs:71,89,106; app/src/user_config/native.rs:74-134 (no keybindings branch); answers doc A1-Q5 #3/#5, A1-Q12`

**Options:**

- **Accept clobber — value-fidelity only (defer)** _(low effort · easy)_ — Keep HashMap + serde_yaml as-is. Scope the 'round-trip safe' promise to binding VALUES only; document that in-app saves rewrite layout (re-order keys, drop comments). Ship no ordering/comment work in Axis 1. (The A1-Q5 atomic write is still required regardless.)
  - 👍 Zero ordering/comment engineering. Values are never lost, only layout; a power user who lives in the file can avoid the in-app editor, and a UI-only user never sees the YAML. Avoids investing in a writer that an A1-Q1 R3b record-schema ruling would rebuild anyway, keeping Axis 1 the 'one-week quick win' the killer question (L125) wants.
  - 👎 Directly contradicts the Axis-1 end-state (file is the version-controlled source of truth, L125/L47): every in-app edit produces an every-line nondeterministic git diff (HashMap reorder, keyboard.rs:169), so the file is unreviewable in VCS and merge-conflict-prone — the exact failure a shareable keymap must avoid. Silent comment loss is a trust violation discovered only after the documentation is gone. Hurts precisely the power-user/team-sharing persona the overhaul targets. Note this option does NOT let you skip work entirely — the A1-Q5 atomic-write data-loss fix is still mandatory on this path.
- **Deterministic canonical order via std BTreeMap (added by red-team)** _(low effort · easy)_ — Swap HashMap -> std::collections::BTreeMap so keys always serialize in a single deterministic (alphabetical) order. After a one-time alphabetization on the first in-app save, every later one-binding edit is a stable, reviewable diff. Pair with the A1-Q5 atomic write. Comments still out of scope.
  - 👍 Cheapest and most robust ordering fix: std-only (no dependency reasoning at all), and BTreeMap::remove preserves order natively, so there is NO indexmap .remove()->.shift_remove() gotcha and no deprecated-API risk. Fully deterministic, so even an external hand-edit that scrambles order gets normalized to a canonical form on next save, REDUCING merge conflicts across machines/teammates. Achieves the core 'reviewable one-line diff' win that is Axis 1's whole point.
  - 👎 Imposes alphabetical order, destroying any hand-authored grouping the user chose for their own file (e.g. all editor bindings together) — a real downside for a config sold as 'yours'. The first in-app save after upgrade rewrites the WHOLE file once (one large diff) before stabilizing, which looks alarming in the very VCS review the feature promises to make clean. Comments still dropped. Slightly worse 'it's your file' story than IndexMap at equal effort.
- **Stable AUTHORED ordering via IndexMap, comments out of scope (RECOMMENDED)** _(low effort · easy)_ ✅ **recommended** — Swap HashMap -> IndexMap so reads preserve the existing file's key order and a one-binding edit changes one line in place (new keys appended at end), giving minimal reviewable git diffs from day one with no reshuffle. Change keyboard.rs:91 .remove() -> .shift_remove() so reset/unbind-to-default also preserves order. Document that in-app saves preserve values + ordering but NOT comments. Pair with the A1-Q5 atomic temp+rename write.
  - 👍 Captures essentially all the version-control value for near-zero cost AND honors the user's authored layout: the format is a flat map (keyboard.rs:155-169) and indexmap+serde is ALREADY a dependency (app/Cargo.toml:133), so the change is a type alias swap plus one .shift_remove() edit, no Cargo change, fully back-compat. Unlike BTreeMap it preserves the user's existing order from the FIRST save (zero reshuffle, strictly minimal diffs immediately, no scary one-time alphabetization). Converts comment-loss from a SILENT clobber into a stated, honest contract. Leaves the door open to full comment fidelity later with no sunk cost. The atomic-write pairing fixes the real data-loss bug on the same path.
  - 👎 Comments are still dropped on in-app save — a user who documents their keymap then clicks one rebind loses the docs; now documented rather than surprising, but still a real loss for the 'file as authored artifact' workflow. The .remove()->.shift_remove() detail is easy to miss and would silently reintroduce a 2-line reshuffle on the reset path if forgotten (and the deprecated alias is a maintenance smell). IndexMap order survives only because there is a single reader/writer today; a future R3b record schema (A1-Q1) re-opens the writer. Does not make file-vs-UI edits non-clobbering at the comment/trivia level.
- **Full round-trip fidelity — comment-preserving surgical merge-in-place** _(high effort · costly)_ — Replace the read-all/serialize-all writer with a comment-and-order-preserving update: edit only the one affected key (surgical line patch for today's flat one-line-per-binding format, or a CST/comment-aware YAML editor), plus an ordered map and atomic temp+rename. An in-app edit then touches only that binding's line and leaves every comment, blank line, and unrelated binding verbatim.
  - 👍 Actually honors the leaf as written — 'file and in-app edits do not clobber each other,' including trivia. The keymap becomes a genuine authored, commented, shareable artifact that survives round-tripping through the UI: the strongest realization of the Axis-1 promise and the most defensible for team-shared dotfiles.
  - 👎 Expensive and brittle. serde_yaml 0.8 cannot preserve comments and is itself deprecated/unmaintained (A1-Q5/Q12; Cargo.lock 0.9.34+deprecated), so this needs a hand-rolled line patch (fragile against quoting, multiline chords, anchors, names containing ':' keyboard.rs:158-159) or a new CST/YAML-editor dependency (likely a migration off serde_yaml). High risk of being thrown away if A1-Q1 rules R3b's record schema into Axis 1 (the serializer is rebuilt). Adds surface area and test burden to what the roadmap wants as a quick win; correctness must be proven against every key-quoting edge case.

**→ Recommendation: Stable AUTHORED ordering via IndexMap, comments out of scope (RECOMMENDED).** The question hides two knobs with wildly different cost/value; the right answer treats them separately and bundles the unrelated-but-co-located atomic-write fix. ORDERING is the high-frequency, universally-visible harm: because CustomKeybindings is a HashMap (keyboard.rs:169) re-serialized whole on every edit (keyboard.rs:71-74,89,115), a one-binding change reshuffles the entire file into an every-line, nondeterministic git diff — defeating the 'version-controlled, reviewable, shareable keymap' that is Axis 1's whole point (L125/L47). Fixing it is near-free: the format is a flat name->string map and indexmap+serde is ALREADY a dependency (app/Cargo.toml:133), so the fix is a type-alias swap plus exactly one call-site change (keyboard.rs:91 .remove() -> .shift_remove()), fully back-compat, no schema change. IndexMap is recommended over the equally-cheap BTreeMap alternative because it preserves the user's existing authored order from the FIRST save (strictly minimal diffs immediately, no one-time full-file alphabetization that would itself produce a scary large diff and overwrite the layout the user chose) — for a file sold as 'yours,' honoring authored order beats imposing a canonical one at identical effort. COMMENTS are the opposite trade: serde_yaml 0.8 has no comment round-trip and is itself deprecated (A1-Q5/Q12), so true fidelity needs a CST library or a brittle surgical patch and risks being discarded if A1-Q1 rules R3b's record schema into Axis 1 — so convert comment loss from a silent clobber into a documented contract ('in-app edits preserve values and ordering, not comments') and defer real fidelity to a fast-follow gated on the A1-Q1 ruling plus dogfood evidence that users actually comment their keymaps. Adopt the A1-Q5 atomic temp+rename write regardless: the truncating create_file over a full-file rewrite is the one genuine runtime data-loss bug here and is independent of formatting. DEFERABILITY: nothing in Phase 1 is strictly blocked by the ordering fix (there is no hot-reload, so this is VCS hygiene not runtime breakage) — but it is so cheap and so directly serves the headline promise that deferring it has poor cost/benefit; the comment half is the genuinely deferrable piece. Option 1 is indefensible against the stated end-state; Option 2 (BTreeMap) is a fine fallback but trades away authored order; Option 4 over-invests ahead of the schema decision.

- _Residual risk:_ The strongest surviving counter: this entire decision is VCS-diff cosmetics, not a runtime bug. Because there is no hot-reload watcher for keybindings.yaml (native.rs:74-134; read once at startup, lib.rs:2557), no user ever experiences runtime breakage from reshuffled order or dropped comments — only an unreviewable git diff. A skeptic can argue the ONLY must-fix on this path is the A1-Q5 atomic write, and that choosing IndexMap vs BTreeMap vs nothing is bikeshedding that could ride the A1-Q1 R3b serializer rebuild for free, avoiding even the trivial sunk cost. The rebuttal is cost-asymmetry, not necessity: the ordering swap is a few lines on a path already being touched for the atomic-write fix, it directly underwrites the L125 'shareable, version-controlled' headline that Axis 1 is sold on, and the sunk cost if R3b later rebuilds the writer is negligible. The residual that does NOT fully dissolve: IndexMap's order guarantee holds only as long as the single-reader/single-writer + no-hot-reload assumptions hold; if A1-Q1 lands R3b in Axis 1 or a hot-reload writer is added that does not also route through the ordered map, the guarantee silently regresses — so this decision is implicitly betting that A1-Q1 defers R3b out of Axis 1.
- _Dependencies:_ Gated by A1-Q1 (does R3b's record schema land in Axis 1?) — if yes, the serializer is rebuilt and any comment-preserving writer built now is at risk, strengthening 'defer comments'; this recommendation implicitly BETS R3b is deferred (if not, the IndexMap order guarantee is re-opened by the new writer). Shares the write path with A1-Q5 (split absent-vs-parse-error reader + atomic temp+rename write — adopt alongside; it is the only runtime data-loss bug here and is independent of the ordering/comment choice). A1-Q12 (legacy flat-map back-compat) — both IndexMap and BTreeMap must keep reading today's flat map; both trivially do. A1-Q6 (echo-suppression on self-write re-firing a watcher) is orthogonal AND moot today since no keybindings watcher exists (native.rs:74-134); a smaller surgical write would not change that a write re-fires a future watcher. A1-Q14 (malformed-file error banner) is independent. Hidden dependency now made explicit: the ordering guarantee assumes a single reader/writer and no hot-reload, both of which hold today (keyboard.rs is the sole structure; no watcher branch), so the guarantee is safe NOW but is contingent on those staying true.
- _What would settle it:_ The ordering half needs no experiment — produce two consecutive in-app saves and diff them; the every-line HashMap reshuffle is self-evident, so ship an ordered map (IndexMap with .shift_remove(), or BTreeMap). Choose IndexMap vs BTreeMap on ONE product question: do we honor the user's hand-authored key order/grouping (IndexMap, no reshuffle) or impose a deterministic canonical order (BTreeMap, one-time alphabetization but std-only and no deprecated-API gotcha)? The comment half is settled by two inputs: (1) product positioning — is 'a commented, documented, team-shareable keymap that survives the UI' a headline promise or a nice-to-have? and (2) dogfood/power-user evidence on whether 'hand-comment the file, then make an in-app tweak' is a real workflow (serde_yaml cannot read comments for telemetry, so inspect shared dotfiles or run a dogfooder survey). Resolve the comment tier only AFTER the A1-Q1 schema ruling so the writer is built once. Separately and unconditionally: adopt the A1-Q5 atomic temp+rename write — that is settled by the existing read-modify-write-with-unwrap_or_default evidence and needs no further input.
- _Red-team added option:_ Deterministic canonical order via std BTreeMap (no new dependency, no remove-semantics gotcha)
- _Red-team:_ Re-opened every cited line; all load-bearing facts hold verbatim. CONFIRMED: HashMap newtype (keyboard.rs:169), serde_yaml 0.8 to_writer whole-map rewrite (:115), read-modify-write with unwrap_or_default on write AND remove (:71,:89), flat name->String map (PersistedTrigger :174), reachable from settings UI (bindings.rs:496 rebind, :513 reset-to-default, keybindings.rs:600 unbind), non-atomic truncating fs::File::create (util/file.rs:143), and the unwrap_or_default clobber data-loss path (cross-checked against settled A1-Q5 #5 and A1-Q12). Three corrections to the brief: (1) HARDEN — indexmap with the serde feature is ALREADY a workspace dependency (app/Cargo.toml:133, resolved 2.12.0), so the recommended swap needs NO Cargo.toml change at all; the brief implied the serde feature would be added. (2) DEFLATE the 'drop-in type swap' framing — it is a type swap PLUS one required call-site change: keyboard.rs:91 map.0.remove(...) (reached only via reset-to-default, bindings.rs:513) is IndexMap::remove which has swap_remove semantics (relocates the last entry, deprecated in indexmap 2.x) and MUST become .shift_remove(), else reset/unbind-to-default still emits a 2-line reshuffle diff. The settings-UI unbind button (keybindings.rs:600) is fine — it INSERTs 'none', not a remove. (3) STAKES nuance the brief overstated as runtime risk — there is NO hot-reload watcher for keybindings.yaml (native.rs:74-134 has branches for themes/workflows/launch_configs/tab_configs/settings only; A1-Q5/Q12 confirm), file is read once at startup (lib.rs:2557) by the single reader/writer (CustomKeybindings is the sole structure), so the ordering harm's real-world impact is unreviewable VCS diffs, NOT live data loss; the only runtime data-loss on this path is the separate A1-Q5 atomic-write bug, which must be fixed regardless of how this question is answered. Added a missing option (BTreeMap). Net: recommendation upheld and strengthened (cheaper than stated), with the shift_remove correctness detail and BTreeMap alternative folded in.

---

## Axis 2 — Scope & context control

### A2-Q1 — HARDENED · _product_ · owner: Keybindings-initiative product owner (PM) decides scope and the user-facing public names, jointly with the warpui_core keymap/engine owner who owns the intern+validation table, the alias-indirection mapping, and the naming-convention RFC. NEW: the Axis-3 owner must co-sign on sequencing, because whether a published vocabulary delivers user value depends on Axis-3 Fixed-remappability and on the per-binding override path (A2-Q11) landing. The docs/catalog owner (Axis-5 R9) is a consumer, not a decider. Not a pure-eng call: it defines the public config API and the names users memorize.

**Decision.** Define the stable, documented, versioned set of context atoms that user-authored keybinding predicates may reference, and fence off the internal flags (the ~454 auto-inserted ui_name() identifiers, transient UI state, test-only literals) that users must not depend on. REFINEMENT from the attack: the published vocabulary should be a curated PUBLIC ALIAS layer (an indirection table mapping a stable public name to one-or-more internal flags), not a bare allowlist of internal names — so internal refactors (the exact concern in the stakes paragraph) update one table entry instead of breaking every user config, and the existing duplicate/inconsistent names collapse to a single public alias. Do the partition + naming RFC NOW (cheap, and the single source of truth for the parser, the A2-Q10 validator, and the Axis-5 R9 catalog), but PUBLISH/FREEZE names incrementally — only once a context-aware override path exists to consume them — so no name becomes a one-way commitment before users can depend on it. Minimal-first set scoped to what is deliverable in Phase 2: cross-view scoping (Terminal vs code/editor vs AI-input). Vim-mode atoms explicitly DEFERRED to the Axis-3 release because the canonical Vim forks are FixedBindings the override path cannot reach today.

**Stakes.** Context-scoped bindings are the headline value of Axis 2, but a predicate is only durable if the flags it names are durable AND only useful if an override path can act on them. Today the namespace is undifferentiated: ~60 inconsistent product set.insert(\"...\") literals PLUS ~454 auto-inserted ui_name() Rust-type identifiers, stored as &'static str with no parser and no public/internal distinction. Exposing this raw makes every user predicate an unversioned coupling to internal names that change on routine refactors — positive atoms fail closed (config silently breaks), negative atoms fail open (bindings leak into wrong views), with none of the engine's positive-gate discipline available to a config author. This is genuinely the foundational API-surface decision and freezing an internal name is a one-way trap. BUT the attack reveals the urgency is lower than the brief implies: the vocabulary delivers no user-visible value on its own. The whole override write path is name-only (set_custom_trigger/update_custom_trigger carry no context), so a scoped override cannot even be created; and the flagship motivating cases (Vim enter/backspace/tab forks) are FixedBindings unreachable without Axis 3. So the real stake is 'design the durable surface and avoid the trap now, but do not pay the freeze cost before a consumer exists.'

_Constrained by:_
- Context is HashSet/HashMap<&'static str> and ContextPredicate (Identifier/Equal/NotEqual/Not/And/Or/Just) has no FromStr/Display/serde; repo-wide grep returns no parser. Any user-facing vocabulary must be interned to static strings (leak/intern precedent exists per A2-Q8) and validated against a hand-maintained table regardless, so an allowlist/alias table is nearly free relative to the mandatory parser. — `crates/warpui_core/src/keymap/context.rs:4-18,100-119`
- Every view auto-inserts its Rust type's ui_name() into the context set at view/mod.rs:116; `grep fn ui_name` returns 456 matches (incl. the trait decl at :60 and a doc example at :52), i.e. ~454 refactor-volatile internal identifiers leak into the namespace automatically. The brief's '383' is an UNDERcount; the coupling liability is larger, strengthening the case against exposing raw names. — `crates/warpui_core/src/core/view/mod.rs:116 + grep `fn ui_name` (456)`
- ~60 distinct product literals with inconsistent naming: bare (Vim, IMEOpen, VoltronActive) vs prefixed (Workspace_SingleTab, PaneGroup_PaneMaximized); the SAME concept under two names (PaneGroup_PaneDragging AND Workspace_PaneDragging both present); exactly one real product map/Equal key (TerminalView_BlockSelectionCardinality). CORRECTION: the a/b/c 'test junk' is confined to context_tests.rs and never enters the runtime namespace, so the brief overstates it. — `grep `set.insert("...")` across app+crates (81 insertions, ~60 distinct); map key app/src/terminal/view.rs:27525`
- Eval is asymmetric per-view: positive atom on a missing/renamed flag fails closed; negative fails open. The engine is safe only by discipline: confirmed 12 real ne!("TerminalView_BlockSelectionCardinality","None") predicates in product, EVERY one ANDed with the positive gate id!("Terminal"). A config author has no way to know this rule, so raw negation is the sharpest footgun. — `crates/warpui_core/src/keymap/context.rs:103-113; A2-Q9 answer (terminal/view/init.rs:385..765)`
- DECISIVE / brief omitted: the entire override write path is name-only. set_custom_trigger(name, trigger) (app.rs:1637) -> update_custom_trigger(name,...) (keymap.rs:422-433) carries no context arg and rewrites ALL same-name bindings; there is no path to create a context-scoped binding or target one same-name binding. The flagship per-context forks (Vim enter/backspace/tab) are FixedBindings (19 sites) the path never touches. So the vocabulary is INERT until (a) a per-binding context-aware override path lands (A2-Q11) and (b) Axis 3 makes Fixed bindings remappable (A2-Q13). — `crates/warpui_core/src/core/app.rs:1637; crates/warpui_core/src/keymap.rs:422-433; A2-Q11/A2-Q13 answers`
- Schema back-compat hazard the brief omitted: config is a flat HashMap<String,PersistedTrigger> with no context field (keyboard.rs:169), and the loader conflates absent-vs-parse-error via unwrap_or_default (keyboard.rs:129-137). Adding the `context?` field (A2-Q3) WITHOUT a back-compat deserializer that splits those cases silently resets every user's keymap on upgrade. — `app/src/keyboard.rs:169,129-137; A1-Q12 answer`
- Vim atoms (Vim/VimNormalMode/VimVisualMode) are inserted only when vim_mode is active, so a referenced atom may simply never appear depending on settings: 'documented' != 'always evaluable'. Combined with the FixedBinding fact, Vim-mode vocabulary is the worst day-one candidate. — `app/src/code/editor/view.rs:2394-2401; app/src/editor/view/mod.rs:8692-8695`

**Options:**

- **Curated public alias layer (indirection table), designed now & published incrementally** _(high effort · easy)_ ✅ **recommended** — Publish stable PUBLIC names the validator accepts, each backed by an indirection table mapping the public name to one-or-more internal flags. The public name is the only versioned surface; internals stay private and renameable. Do the partition + naming RFC now; freeze/publish names incrementally, only as a consuming override path lands. Minimal-first set = cross-view scopes deliverable without Axis 3 (Terminal, code/editor, AI-input). Vim-mode atoms deferred to the Axis-3 release.
  - 👍 Directly defuses the one-way trap the stakes paragraph is about: a refactor that renames a Rust type updates ONE table entry, not a frozen public API, so configs survive. It collapses existing inconsistencies for free (PaneGroup_PaneDragging and Workspace_PaneDragging become one public alias). You must build the intern+validate table anyway (context.rs:4-18, no FromStr), so the indirection is marginal cost over the mandatory parser. Designing now but freezing later means you never pay the costly-reversibility commitment before a consumer (A2-Q11 override path / Axis 3) can exercise the name, capturing D's restraint with A's correctness. Single source of truth for parser + A2-Q10 validator + Axis-5 R9 catalog; tag each alias mode-vs-flag so A2-Q9 negation is safe by construction.
  - 👎 An alias table is itself a maintenance surface and a skew risk: an alias pointing at a renamed/removed internal flag is silent breakage unless a compile-time check binds aliases to live flag constants. Slightly more design than a bare allowlist. Still requires the partition/RFC and mode-vs-flag tagging. And it does not by itself make any scoped override WORK: it is necessary-but-not-sufficient (needs A2-Q11 per-binding override path + Axis 3 for the flagship cases).
- **Curated bare-name allowlist (original recommendation)** _(high effort · costly)_ — Publish a subset of the existing internal atom strings as the stable set; the validator accepts only those. Minimal-first; ui_name internals excluded unless promoted.
  - 👍 Cheaper than the alias layer and the published names can literally be the strings already in code, so no mapping to maintain. Still kills the B-trap by rejecting the ~454 ui_name names and tags modes vs flags for safe negation.
  - 👎 Publishing internal names freezes them: you can no longer rename Terminal, EditorView, etc., because users now type them, exactly the one-way commitment the stakes paragraph warns against, for a curated subset. Does not resolve the duplicate-concept names without an alias anyway. This is why the alias variant strictly dominates on reversibility.
- **Expose all flags as-is (raw flag soup)** _(low effort · one-way)_ — Allow any currently-inserted flag including ~454 ui_name() identifiers and the ad-hoc literals, no allowlist.
  - 👍 Cheapest; ships immediately; maximal power; zero curation backlog.
  - 👎 The trap the question warns against: makes ~454 internal Rust type names plus inconsistent literals a permanent versioned public API. Refactors silently break configs (positive fail-closed) or leak bindings into wrong views (negative fail-open, none of the engine's positive-gate discipline available to authors). Conditional Vim flags appear/vanish with settings. One-way: once users depend on a ui_name() string you can never rename a view type. Confirmed worse than the brief stated (~454 names, not 383).
- **Two-tier: stable core + explicit unstable escape hatch** _(very-high effort · costly)_ — Curated stable tier PLUS an opt-in `unstable:` namespace exposing raw flags with a no-guarantee marker.
  - 👍 Serves power users without gating release on perfect curation; unstable usage becomes a promotion signal.
  - 👎 Most design/UI/docs work of all options. Users treat 'unstable' as stable; raw flags still break and generate support load. Two validation paths. It only relabels the ui_name() volatility rather than solving it. The FixedBinding finding makes it worse: you would expose unstable raw flags that STILL cannot override the Vim forks, so the escape hatch buys power users almost nothing for the motivating cases.
- **Defer - global/per-name overrides only, no context vocabulary in Phase 2** _(low effort · easy)_ — Ship name-keyed rebinding (which nearly exists today); reserve `context?` in the schema but do not honor it. Revisit the vocabulary when the partition RFC and the per-binding override path land.
  - 👍 Removes the coupling risk entirely from the first release; cheapest correct option. Re-priced UP by the attack: the brief's 'kills the headline value prop' is largely false because the headline value (Vim forks) is ALREADY blocked on Axis 3 and on the missing per-binding override path, so deferring the vocabulary forfeits little real Phase-2 value. Buys time to do the RFC properly and measure which scopes users actually request before freezing any name.
  - 👎 Does not answer the question, only postpones it; if Axis 2 context-scoping IS in Phase 2 you still need the vocabulary. Must reserve `context?` now (A2-Q3) WITH the A1-Q12 back-compat deserializer, or upgrade silently resets user keymaps and you face a second migration. Even reserved, the partition design work is unavoidable eventually.

**→ Recommendation: Curated public alias layer (indirection table), designed now & published incrementally.** The direction is upheld and strengthened: exposing raw flags (B) is a confirmed one-way trap over ~454 ui_name names (larger than the brief's 383), and you must build an intern+validate table regardless (context.rs:4-18, no FromStr), so a curated surface is the cheap path. But the brief's bare-allowlist still admits 'the names you publish are now frozen', and that frozen commitment is precisely the stakes-paragraph trap. The alias-indirection variant resolves it: only the public name is versioned, internals stay renameable, and the duplicate/inconsistent names (PaneGroup_PaneDragging vs Workspace_PaneDragging) collapse to one alias for marginal extra cost. The hardening that matters most is sequencing. The attack establishes (app.rs:1637; keymap.rs:422-433; A2-Q11/Q13) that the override write path is name-only and the flagship Vim forks are FixedBindings the path cannot touch, so the vocabulary delivers ZERO user value until a per-binding override path (A2-Q11) and Axis 3 Fixed-remappability land. Therefore: do the partition + naming RFC NOW (cheap, prevents the trap, single source of truth for parser/validator/catalog), but PUBLISH/FREEZE names incrementally and only when a consumer exists, capturing D's restraint without throwing away A's design. Scope the first published set to contexts actually deliverable in Phase 2 (Terminal vs code/editor vs AI-input) and explicitly DEFER the Vim-mode atoms to the Axis-3 release, since publishing a Vim vocabulary users cannot exercise is a frozen API with no payoff. Two-tier (C) is premium effort that only relabels the coupling and still cannot reach the Fixed forks. If the program decides Axis 2 context-scoping slips out of Phase 2 entirely, fall back to D; it now costs almost nothing in real value.

- _Residual risk:_ The strongest surviving counter is that the alias indirection trades one maintenance burden for another: an alias pointing at a renamed or deleted internal flag is silent breakage unless a compile-time binding (aliases reference the flag constant, not a loose string) is enforced, so the 'internals stay renameable' benefit only holds if that check is actually built; otherwise you have reintroduced the coupling with extra indirection. Second, minimal-first scoped to non-Vim contexts may ship a vocabulary so thin (bind a key only in Terminal vs editor) that it has no compelling Phase-2 story once the motivating Vim cases are removed, at which point the honest call is full defer (D) until the Axis-3 release bundles vocabulary + per-binding override + Fixed-remappability into one shippable, demonstrably useful feature. The recommendation is correct conditional on Axis 2 context-scoping being a Phase-2 deliverable; if it is not, D dominates.
- _Dependencies:_ Co-decided with A2-Q8 (no FromStr + &'static str storage; the parser must intern user strings/aliases and validate against this table). Directly gates A2-Q10 (the allowlist/alias set IS what Q10 validates) and A2-Q6 (grammar). Interacts with A2-Q9 (tag atoms mode-vs-flag so `!`/`!=` is safe; the 12 product ne! sites prove the positive-gate discipline authors won't know). NEWLY EXPOSED gating consumers the brief omitted: A2-Q11 (the override write path is name-only; without a per-binding context-aware path the vocabulary is inert) and A2-Q13 / Axis 3 (the flagship Vim forks are FixedBindings unreachable until Fixed-remappability lands). Schema dependency on A2-Q3 (the `context?` field) is real AND inherits the A1-Q12 hazard: the back-compat deserializer must split absent-vs-parse-error or upgrade silently resets every user's keymap. Feeds A2-Q4 (override targeting) and Axis-5 R9 (catalog).
- _What would settle it:_ 1) SEQUENCING decision (new, decisive): is Axis 2 context-scoping a Phase-2 deliverable given the flagship cases need Axis 3 + a per-binding override path?
